I don’t know if I’m answering it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a sense of loss of control during these nighttime eating episodes, like you can't stop eating once you start, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m just going to have one thing, or I’ll stop after this, but once I start it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is kind of watching from the outside, saying, “Joan, stop, this is enough.” And then… my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth anyway.\n\nI don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so dramatic, but it really does feel like I can’t stop until I’m painfully full. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you’ve already gone to bed and maybe even fallen asleep, or are you still up and about when they start?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think it’s usually like I’ve gone to sleep and then wake up to eat, if that’s what you mean.\n\nMost of the time I’m still up—like finishing schoolwork or trying to unwind—and then it kind of starts from there. But I guess my schedule gets blurry at night, so I don’t want to say it too confidently if that matters.\n\nDoctor: So you mentioned you're usually still up when these episodes start, but do you ever wake up in the middle of the night and find yourself eating without really being aware of it or remembering it clearly afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way.\n\nI mean, I might be up really late and feel kind of foggy or embarrassed about how much time has passed, but I don’t usually wake up and find food out or realize I ate without knowing it. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIs that what you mean—like sleepwalking kind of eating? Because I don’t think that’s what’s happening for me.\n\nDoctor: During these nighttime eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much more rapidly than usual, like eating quickly without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really paid attention to the speed, honestly.\n\nI mean… I don’t sit there calmly like it’s a normal snack, but I don’t know if I’d say “much more rapidly” for sure. It’s more like I’m not thinking very clearly about what I’m doing, if that makes sense. I can try to notice that more, though, if that would be helpful.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s embarrassing to admit, but yes, it feels like I can’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes cause you a lot of distress or affect your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, probably more than I like to admit. But my evenings and weekends kind of end up revolving around… making up for whatever happened with food the night before, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then I’m slower with grading, or I’m not really present when I’m with friends because I’m worrying about it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more like it starts with a specific moment, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be done grading or trying to wind down, and I’ll go get leftovers, and then it turns into… more than I meant it to.

It doesn’t feel like the whole evening exactly. It’s more like once I start, I keep going—cereal, crackers, peanut butter, sometimes the snacks I packed for school—and then eventually I stop because I realize how much it’s been. I don’t know if that answers it the right way, but it does feel like there’s a “before” and “after,” kind of.

I mean, I don’t want to exaggerate, but it’s not just like a normal snack. I’ll finish grading and start with some leftovers, and then within the evening I’ve gone through cereal, crackers, peanut butter… sometimes even the snacks I meant to take for my students or my classroom. \n\nIt feels like a lot for that amount of time, and afterward I’m kind of shocked by how much it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry when these nighttime eating episodes start, or do you find yourself eating even when you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure, honestly. description: The binge eating and inappropriate compensatory behaviors both occur, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: For the past few months, there has rarely been a week without at least one night where I eat far more than I meant to, and the next day I try to make up for it by not eating at school or pushing myself through a long walk or extra yoga.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: My evenings and weekends feel planned around undoing what happened with food, so I’ve been slower with grading, less present with friends, and constantly worried about seeming put-together at work.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about four months, since I I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s kind of why I’m here.\n\nThere are nights where I’ll start with, like, leftovers after I’ve been grading papers, and then it just keeps going—cereal, crackers, peanut butter, snacks I had packed for my classroom… and afterward I’m scared by how much it was. So yes, I think it is more than most people would eat in that amount of time.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if these nighttime eating episodes usually have a clear beginning and end, like a specific moment you start and then a point where you stop, or does it feel more like a general pattern that stretches across the whole evening?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like it starts with a specific moment, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be done grading or trying to wind down, and I’ll go get leftovers, and then it turns into… more than I meant it to.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like the whole evening exactly. It’s more like once I start, I keep going—cereal, crackers, peanut butter, sometimes the snacks I packed for school—and then eventually I stop because I realize how much it’s been. I don’t know if that answers it the right way, but it does feel like there’s a “before” and “after,” kind of.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify whether these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you've already fallen asleep earlier that night, or do they typically start while you're still awake and active?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I don’t think I can say for sure. Yeah… I think it does, more than I’ve wanted to admit.

I still get to work and I think I probably look like I’m managing okay, but it takes a lot of effort to seem put-together. My evenings and weekends end up kind of revolving around… fixing or undoing what happened with food, if that makes sense. And then grading takes me longer, or I’m slower getting things done at home.

I’ve also been less present with friends. I’ll answer texts late or avoid plans because I’m either worried about food or trying to get myself back on track. I don’t know if that counts as “functioning,” but it definitely feels like it’s taking up way more space than it should.

I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I guess I’m here because my eating has been getting kind of out of control at night. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but after I’m done grading papers I’ll start with leftovers, and then somehow I end up eating cereal, crackers, peanut butter… even snacks I packed for my classroom. It just feels like a really large amount, and it scares me a little. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop eating or control what or how much you're eating during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I try to tell myself I’m just having a snack, but once I start it’s like the reasonable teacher part of me is sort of watching from the outside, saying, “Joan, stop, this is enough.” And I still just… keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I’m painfully full. It’s embarrassing to admit, but yes, it feels like I can’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes cause you a lot of distress or affect your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, probably more than I like to admit. I still get to work and I try really hard to look like everything is fine, so I don’t think people would necessarily know. But my evenings and weekends kind of end up revolving around… making up for whatever happened with food the night before, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then I’m slower with grading, or I’m not really present when I’m with friends because I’m worrying about it. I’m also constantly trying to seem put-together at school, which is exhausting. I don’t know if that counts as “affecting daily life,” but… it feels like it does.\n\nDoctor: When you have these nighttime eating episodes, would you say you’re eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within a couple of hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I mean, I don’t want to exaggerate, but it’s not just like a normal snack. I’ll finish grading and start with some leftovers, and then within the evening I’ve gone through cereal, crackers, peanut butter… sometimes even the snacks I meant to take for my students or my classroom. \n\nIt feels like a lot for that amount of time, and afterward I’m kind of shocked by how much it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry when these nighttime eating episodes start, or do you find yourself eating even when you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure, honestly. Sometimes I tell myself I’m hungry, like I deserve dinner or a snack after a long day, and maybe at the very beginning I am a little. But other times it’s more like… I just start eating because I’m tired or keyed up, not because my stomach is actually hungry.\n\nDoes that make sense? I don’t know if I’m answering it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a sense of loss of control during these nighttime eating episodes, like you can't stop eating once you start, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m just going to have one thing, or I’ll stop after this, but once I start it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is kind of watching from the outside, saying, “Joan, stop, this is enough.” And then… my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth anyway.\n\nI don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so dramatic, but it really does feel like I can’t stop until I’m painfully full. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you’ve already gone to bed and maybe even fallen asleep, or are you still up and about when they start?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think it’s usually like I’ve gone to sleep and then wake up to eat, if that’s what you mean.\n\nMost of the time I’m still up—like finishing schoolwork or trying to unwind—and then it kind of starts from there. But I guess my schedule gets blurry at night, so I don’t want to say it too confidently if that matters.\n\nDoctor: So you mentioned you're usually still up when these episodes start, but do you ever wake up in the middle of the night and find yourself eating without really being aware of it or remembering it clearly afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way.\n\nI mean, I might be up really late and feel kind of foggy or embarrassed about how much time has passed, but I don’t usually wake up and find food out or realize I ate without knowing it. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIs that what you mean—like sleepwalking kind of eating? Because I don’t think that’s what’s happening for me.\n\nDoctor: During these nighttime eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much more rapidly than usual, like eating quickly without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really paid attention to the speed, honestly.\n\nI mean… I don’t sit there calmly like it’s a normal snack, but I don’t know if I’d say “much more rapidly” for sure. It’s more like I’m not thinking very clearly about what I’m doing, if that makes sense. I can try to notice that more, though, if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: During these nighttime eating episodes, do you find yourself eating even when you don’t feel physically hungry?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I don’t always check in with myself that way in the moment.\n\nSometimes it doesn’t feel like normal hunger, exactly, but I don’t know if I can say that confidently. It’s more like I just end up eating and then think about it afterward. Is that an okay way to answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these nighttime episodes because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know if it’s because I’m embarrassed, exactly.\n\nA lot of the time I am alone, but it’s also nighttime, so that just sort of happens. I don’t think I’m making a clear decision like, “I need to hide this.” Or… maybe I don’t want anyone to see it, but I’m not sure how much of that is embarrassment versus just wanting to be left alone.\n\nIs that too vague? I’m sorry—I just don’t want to say yes if I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do your nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you’ve already fallen asleep at some point earlier that night?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, sometimes it feels like I’ve been asleep already and then I’m up, but I don’t know if I could say that’s usually how it happens.\n\nI haven’t really tracked it closely… I’m sorry. I can try to pay more attention to whether I’d already fallen asleep first, if that would help.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the middle of one of these nighttime eating episodes, would you say you’re eating a large amount of food in a short period—like within a couple of hours—more than most people would eat in that same timeframe?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. But my evenings and weekends kind of end up revolving around… making up for whatever happened with food the night before, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then I’m slower with grading, or I’m not really present when I’m with friends because I’m worrying about it. I’m also constantly trying to seem put-together at school, which is exhausting. I don’t know if that counts as “affecting daily life,” but… it feels like it does.\n\nDoctor: When you have these nighttime eating episodes, would you say you’re eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within a couple of hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I mean, I don’t want to exaggerate, but it’s not just like a normal snack. I’ll finish grading and start with some leftovers, and then within the evening I’ve gone through cereal, crackers, peanut butter… sometimes even the snacks I meant to take for my students or my classroom. \n\nIt feels like a lot for that amount of time, and afterward I’m kind of shocked by how much it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry when these nighttime eating episodes start, or do you find yourself eating even when you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure, honestly. Sometimes I tell myself I’m hungry, like I deserve dinner or a snack after a long day, and maybe at the very beginning I am a little. But other times it’s more like… I just start eating because I’m tired or keyed up, not because my stomach is actually hungry.\n\nDoes that make sense? I don’t know if I’m answering it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a sense of loss of control during these nighttime eating episodes, like you can't stop eating once you start, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m just going to have one thing, or I’ll stop after this, but once I start it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is kind of watching from the outside, saying, “Joan, stop, this is enough.” And then… my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth anyway.\n\nI don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so dramatic, but it really does feel like I can’t stop until I’m painfully full. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you’ve already gone to bed and maybe even fallen asleep, or are you still up and about when they start?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think it’s usually like I’ve gone to sleep and then wake up to eat, if that’s what you mean.\n\nMost of the time I’m still up—like finishing schoolwork or trying to unwind—and then it kind of starts from there. But I guess my schedule gets blurry at night, so I don’t want to say it too confidently if that matters.\n\nDoctor: So you mentioned you're usually still up when these episodes start, but do you ever wake up in the middle of the night and find yourself eating without really being aware of it or remembering it clearly afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way.\n\nI mean, I might be up really late and feel kind of foggy or embarrassed about how much time has passed, but I don’t usually wake up and find food out or realize I ate without knowing it. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIs that what you mean—like sleepwalking kind of eating? Because I don’t think that’s what’s happening for me.\n\nDoctor: During these nighttime eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much more rapidly than usual, like eating quickly without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really paid attention to the speed, honestly.\n\nI mean… I don’t sit there calmly like it’s a normal snack, but I don’t know if I’d say “much more rapidly” for sure. It’s more like I’m not thinking very clearly about what I’m doing, if that makes sense. I can try to notice that more, though, if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: During these nighttime eating episodes, do you find yourself eating even when you don’t feel physically hungry?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I don’t always check in with myself that way in the moment.\n\nSometimes it doesn’t feel like normal hunger, exactly, but I don’t know if I can say that confidently. It’s more like I just end up eating and then think about it afterward. Is that an okay way to answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these nighttime episodes because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know if it’s because I’m embarrassed, exactly.\n\nA lot of the time I am alone, but it’s also nighttime, so that just sort of happens. I don’t think I’m making a clear decision like, “I need to hide this.” Or… maybe I don’t want anyone to see it, but I’m not sure how much of that is embarrassment versus just wanting to be left alone.\n\nIs that too vague? I’m sorry—I just don’t want to say yes if I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do your nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you’ve already fallen asleep at some point earlier that night?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, sometimes it feels like I’ve been asleep already and then I’m up, but I don’t know if I could say that’s usually how it happens.\n\nI haven’t really tracked it closely… I’m sorry. I can try to pay more attention to whether I’d already fallen asleep first, if that would help.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the middle of one of these nighttime eating episodes, would you say you’re eating a large amount of food in a short period—like within a couple of hours—more than most people would eat in that same timeframe?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s kind of why I’m here.\n\nThere are nights where I’ll start with, like, leftovers after I’ve been grading papers, and then it just keeps going—cereal, crackers, peanut butter, snacks I had packed for my classroom… and afterward I’m scared by how much it was. So yes, I think it is more than most people would eat in that amount of time.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if these nighttime eating episodes usually have a clear beginning and end, like a specific moment you start and then a point where you stop, or does it feel more like a general pattern that stretches across the whole evening?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like it starts with a specific moment, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be done grading or trying to wind down, and I’ll go get leftovers, and then it turns into… more than I meant it to.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like the whole evening exactly. It’s more like once I start, I keep going—cereal, crackers, peanut butter, sometimes the snacks I packed for school—and then eventually I stop because I realize how much it’s been. I don’t know if that answers it the right way, but it does feel like there’s a “before” and “after,” kind of.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify whether these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you've already fallen asleep earlier that night, or do they typically start while you're still awake and active?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I don’t think I can say for sure. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer.\n\nMy impression is that a lot of the time I’m still awake—like I’m grading or trying to settle down—and then it starts. But there are also nights where it feels kind of blurry, like I may have dozed off first and then I’m up again. I haven’t paid close enough attention to be confident.\n\nIs it okay if I say I’m not totally sure, but I can try to notice that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if these nighttime eating episodes make it hard for you to function during the day, like affecting your concentration, energy, or ability to manage your usual responsibilities at work or home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, more than I’ve wanted to admit.\n\nI still get to work and I think I probably look like I’m managing okay, but it takes a lot of effort to seem put-together. My evenings and weekends end up kind of revolving around… fixing or undoing what happened with food, if that makes sense. And then grading takes me longer, or I’m slower getting things done at home.\n\nI’ve also been less present with friends. I’ll answer texts late or avoid plans because I’m either worried about food or trying to get myself back on track. I don’t know if that counts as “functioning,” but it definitely feels like it’s taking up way more space than it should.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify whether these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you’ve already had some sleep earlier that night, or do they typically start while you're still fully awake and active?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I feel like I should know that, but I haven’t really tracked it closely.\n\nMost of what I remember is that I’m still awake—like I’m finishing work stuff or just trying to relax—and then I end up in the kitchen. But there are some nights where I’m tired enough that it’s kind of hard to tell if I’d actually fallen asleep first or just sort of zoned out.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say the wrong thing. I think it usually starts while I’m awake, but I’m not 100% certain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m sorry, I know I’m not giving you a very clean answer.

I think… most of the time it’s before I’ve actually gone to bed, like I’m still awake and up. But I don’t want to overstate it, because there are nights where I’m so tired that it’s blurry, and I’m not totally sure if I had dozed off first or not.

So my best answer is: usually while I’m still awake, but I’m not completely certain.

I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I guess I’m here because my eating has been getting kind of out of control at night. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but after I’m done grading papers I’ll start with leftovers, and then somehow I end up eating cereal, crackers, peanut butter… even snacks I packed for my classroom. It just feels like a really large amount, and it scares me a little. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop eating or control what or how much you're eating during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I try to tell myself I’m just having a snack, but once I start it’s like the reasonable teacher part of me is sort of watching from the outside, saying, “Joan, stop, this is enough.” And I still just… keep opening things and putting food in my mouth until I’m painfully full. It’s embarrassing to admit, but yes, it feels like I can’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes cause you a lot of distress or affect your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, probably more than I like to admit. I still get to work and I try really hard to look like everything is fine, so I don’t think people would necessarily know. But my evenings and weekends kind of end up revolving around… making up for whatever happened with food the night before, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then I’m slower with grading, or I’m not really present when I’m with friends because I’m worrying about it. I’m also constantly trying to seem put-together at school, which is exhausting. I don’t know if that counts as “affecting daily life,” but… it feels like it does.\n\nDoctor: When you have these nighttime eating episodes, would you say you’re eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within a couple of hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I mean, I don’t want to exaggerate, but it’s not just like a normal snack. I’ll finish grading and start with some leftovers, and then within the evening I’ve gone through cereal, crackers, peanut butter… sometimes even the snacks I meant to take for my students or my classroom. \n\nIt feels like a lot for that amount of time, and afterward I’m kind of shocked by how much it was.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry when these nighttime eating episodes start, or do you find yourself eating even when you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure, honestly. Sometimes I tell myself I’m hungry, like I deserve dinner or a snack after a long day, and maybe at the very beginning I am a little. But other times it’s more like… I just start eating because I’m tired or keyed up, not because my stomach is actually hungry.\n\nDoes that make sense? I don’t know if I’m answering it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a sense of loss of control during these nighttime eating episodes, like you can't stop eating once you start, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m just going to have one thing, or I’ll stop after this, but once I start it feels like the reasonable teacher part of me is kind of watching from the outside, saying, “Joan, stop, this is enough.” And then… my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth anyway.\n\nI don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so dramatic, but it really does feel like I can’t stop until I’m painfully full. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you’ve already gone to bed and maybe even fallen asleep, or are you still up and about when they start?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think it’s usually like I’ve gone to sleep and then wake up to eat, if that’s what you mean.\n\nMost of the time I’m still up—like finishing schoolwork or trying to unwind—and then it kind of starts from there. But I guess my schedule gets blurry at night, so I don’t want to say it too confidently if that matters.\n\nDoctor: So you mentioned you're usually still up when these episodes start, but do you ever wake up in the middle of the night and find yourself eating without really being aware of it or remembering it clearly afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way.\n\nI mean, I might be up really late and feel kind of foggy or embarrassed about how much time has passed, but I don’t usually wake up and find food out or realize I ate without knowing it. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIs that what you mean—like sleepwalking kind of eating? Because I don’t think that’s what’s happening for me.\n\nDoctor: During these nighttime eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much more rapidly than usual, like eating quickly without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really paid attention to the speed, honestly.\n\nI mean… I don’t sit there calmly like it’s a normal snack, but I don’t know if I’d say “much more rapidly” for sure. It’s more like I’m not thinking very clearly about what I’m doing, if that makes sense. I can try to notice that more, though, if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: During these nighttime eating episodes, do you find yourself eating even when you don’t feel physically hungry?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I don’t always check in with myself that way in the moment.\n\nSometimes it doesn’t feel like normal hunger, exactly, but I don’t know if I can say that confidently. It’s more like I just end up eating and then think about it afterward. Is that an okay way to answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these nighttime episodes because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know if it’s because I’m embarrassed, exactly.\n\nA lot of the time I am alone, but it’s also nighttime, so that just sort of happens. I don’t think I’m making a clear decision like, “I need to hide this.” Or… maybe I don’t want anyone to see it, but I’m not sure how much of that is embarrassment versus just wanting to be left alone.\n\nIs that too vague? I’m sorry—I just don’t want to say yes if I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do your nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you’ve already fallen asleep at some point earlier that night?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, sometimes it feels like I’ve been asleep already and then I’m up, but I don’t know if I could say that’s usually how it happens.\n\nI haven’t really tracked it closely… I’m sorry. I can try to pay more attention to whether I’d already fallen asleep first, if that would help.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the middle of one of these nighttime eating episodes, would you say you’re eating a large amount of food in a short period—like within a couple of hours—more than most people would eat in that same timeframe?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s kind of why I’m here.\n\nThere are nights where I’ll start with, like, leftovers after I’ve been grading papers, and then it just keeps going—cereal, crackers, peanut butter, snacks I had packed for my classroom… and afterward I’m scared by how much it was. So yes, I think it is more than most people would eat in that amount of time.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if these nighttime eating episodes usually have a clear beginning and end, like a specific moment you start and then a point where you stop, or does it feel more like a general pattern that stretches across the whole evening?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like it starts with a specific moment, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be done grading or trying to wind down, and I’ll go get leftovers, and then it turns into… more than I meant it to.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like the whole evening exactly. It’s more like once I start, I keep going—cereal, crackers, peanut butter, sometimes the snacks I packed for school—and then eventually I stop because I realize how much it’s been. I don’t know if that answers it the right way, but it does feel like there’s a “before” and “after,” kind of.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify whether these nighttime eating episodes usually happen after you've already fallen asleep earlier that night, or do they typically start while you're still awake and active?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I don’t think I can say for sure. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer.\n\nMy impression is that a lot of the time I’m still awake—like I’m grading or trying to settle down—and then it starts. But there are also nights where it feels kind of blurry, like I may have dozed off first and then I’m up again. I haven’t paid close enough attention to be confident.\n\nIs it okay if I say I’m not totally sure, but I can try to notice that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if these nighttime eating episodes make it hard for you to function during the day, like affecting your concentration, energy, or ability to manage your usual responsibilities at work or home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, more than I’ve wanted to admit.\n\nI still get to work and I think I probably look like I’m managing okay, but it takes a lot of effort to seem put-together.       "Episodes not exclusively during sleep (i.e., not sleepwalking or arousal disorder eating)",
      "Eating episodes occur during wakefulness, often with impaired control and distress"
    ]
  }
}
